강의 개요: In this lecture, thermodynamic properties, the first and second laws of thermodynamics are introduced. Based on these, applications for thermodynamics such as gas power cycles, vapor and combined power cycles, and refrigeration cycles are discussed.

Text: Cengel and Boles, Thermodynamics-An Engineering Approach 4th Ed. McGraw-Hill

Reference: 1. Black and Hartley, Thermodynamics, SI Version, 3<sup>rd</sup> Ed., Harper Collins

- 2. Sonntag et al., Fundamentals of Thermodynamics 5th Ed., Wiley
- 3. Wark, Thermodynamics, 6<sup>th</sup> Ed., McGraw-Hill

### Schedule

- 12. 22: Basic Concepts of Thermodynamics
- 12. 23: Properties of Pure Substances
- 12. 24: Ideal-Gas Equation of State
- 12. 27: Energy Transfer (Heat, Work, and Mass)
- 12. 28: The First Law of Thermodynamics: Closed Systems
- 12. 29: The First Law of Thermodynamics: Control Volumes
- 12. 30: The Second Law of Thermodynamics
- 12. 31: The Concept of Entropy

- 1. 8: Refrigeration Cycles

2-6. Compressibility factor

This deviation from ideal-gas behavior at a given temperature and pressure can accurately be accounted for the introduction of correction factor called the compressibility factor  $Z = \frac{PV}{RT} = \frac{Vactual}{Videal}$ 

If z=1, it is ideal gas, the father away z=is from unity. The more the gas deviates from ideal gas behavior

Gas behave differently at a given temperature and pressure but they behave very much the same at temperature and pressure normalized with respect to their critical temp, and pressure.  $P_{R} = \frac{P}{Por} \ c \ \text{Reduced pressure}) \quad T_{R} = \frac{T}{Tor} \ c \ \text{Reduced temperature})$ 

[ Fig 2-51]

The principle of corresponding states (45 stat 42):

the Z factor for all gases is approximately the same at the same reduced pressure and the temperature.

chapter 5. The second Law of thermodynamics

- The second law of thermodynamics, which asserts that processes occur in a certain direction and that energy has quality as well as quantity, is introduced.
- The thermal energy <u>reservoirs</u>, reversible and irreversible processes, <u>heat engines</u>, refrigerators, and heat pumps are introduced
- The carnot orde is introduced.

5-1. Introduction to the Second Law

- Satisfying the first law alone does not ensure that the process will actually take place.
- The first law places no restriction on the direction of process.
- The inadequacy of the first law to identify whether a process, can take place is remedied by introducing another general principle, the second law.

| 5-3. Heat Engines (Heat -> work)                                                                         |

- Work can be converted to heat directly and completely but converting heat to work requires the use of some special device -> Heat engine.

Heat

characteristic of Heat engines.

- 1. They reactive heat from a high-temperature source
- 2. They convert part of the heat to work
- 3. They reject the remaining waste heat to a low-temperature sink.
  4. They approte on a cycle.

Working third  $C_{3}^{2}=f_{3}^{2}m_{1}$ ) is used to transfer heat from and to heat engines. Thermal Efficiency is the fraction of the heat input to not work output and physically means the performance of heat engines.  $1 + h = \frac{W_{net}}{Q_{H}} = \frac{Q_{H}}{Q_{H}} = 1 - \frac{Q_{L}}{Q_{H}}$ 

- A heat engine gicle can not be completed without requiring some heat to a low temperature sink
- Every heat engine must waste some energy by transferring it to a low-temperature reservoir in order to complete the cycle, ever under idealized conditions.

The Second Law of Hermody namics: Kelvin - Planck statement

- "It is impossible for any device that operates on a cycle to receive heat from a single reservoir and produce a net amount of work!"
- This physically means that no heat engine can have a thermal efficiency of 100%, or as far a power plant to operate, the working fluid must exchange heat with the environment as well as the furnace

5-5. Refrigirators and Heat pumps

- The transfer of heat from a low temperature medium to a high-temperature one requires special devices called refrigerators.
- Petrigorators, like heat engines, are cycle device
- the working fluid used in the refrigeration cycle is called a refrigerant Coefficient of performance (COP)
  - The efficiency of a refrigerator is expressed in terms of the coefficient of performance (COP), denoted by COPE

$$COP_R = \frac{perined output}{Reguired Input} = \frac{QL}{Whot, in} = \frac{1}{QH/QL}$$

What = QH - QL

Heat Pump

- The second Law of Thermodynamics: Clausius Statement which is related to refrigerators and heat pump
- "It is impossible to construct a device that operates in a cycle and produces no effect other than the transfer of heat from a low-temperature body to a high-temperature body"
- Both the belvin Planck and the clausions statements of the second how are negative statement
- The Kelvin-Plank and clausius statement are equivalent in their consequence.

- A special class of idealized process, called isentropic (entropy is constant during processes) processes are examined

Chapter 8. Gas Power Gycle

Two important areas of application for thermodynamics are power generation and refrigeration. Thermodynamics cycles can be categorized as gas cycles and vapor cycles, depending on the phase of the working fluid.

Yapor gicle: Gasseous phase throughout the entire gicle

Vapor gicle: The working fluid exists in the vapor phase during one part of gicle and in the figured phase during another part.

In this chapter, various gas power orders are analyzed under some simply fying accumptions

- the otto cycle is ideal-cycle for grank-ignition engine.
- the brosel gicke is " for compression-ignition engine.
- The total cycles are internally reversible.
  - a. The cycle does not involve any friction

Development of gas Turbine

- The early gas turbine built in the 19405 and even 1950s had simple-ode efficiencies of about 11% because of the low compressor and turbine efficiencies and

The efforts to improve the cycle efficiency concentrated in these area.

- 1) Increasing the turbine inlet temperature \$40°C -> 14.20°C
- 2) Increasing the efficiencies of turbomachinery components
  (To design turbine and compressor with <u>minimal losses</u> aerodynamically)
- 3) Adding matifications to the basis cycles

- The reason for decreasing the compressor work by wing intercooler and increasing the turbine work by using reheaters are explained as follows:

- The working fluid, which leaves compressor I, enters an intercooler and the specific volume of it is decreased by the cooling effect of the intercooler. The working fluid enters again compressor I to obtain the high pressure. As a result, the compression work is decreased.
- As the number of stages is increased, the compression process becomes hearly isothermal at the compression met temperature and the compression work decreases.
- The work output of a turbine operating between two pressure level an be increased by expanding the gas in dages and reheating it in between that is, utilizing multistage expansor with reheating.
- The book output to a two stage compressor is minimized when equal pressure ratios are maintained across the each stage.  $\frac{P_6}{P_1} = \frac{P_6}{P_1} = \frac{P_8}{P_9}$

8-11. Ideal Jet-Propulsion gale

- Gas turbine engines are widely used to power aircraft because they are light and compact and have a high power-to-weight ratio.
- Africant gas turbines operate on an open cycle called a jet-propulsion cycle.
- Air is compressed by the compressor
- It is mixed with fuel in the compustion damper, where the mixture is burned at constant pressure.
- High pressure and high temperature gases partially expand in the turbine producing enough power to drive the compressor and the other equipment.

   Finally the gases expand in a nozzle to the ambient prossure and leave the aircraft at a high velocity.

the Harust (2753) in a turbine engine is the unbalanced force that is caused by the difference in the momentum of the low-velocity air entering the engine and the high velocity exhaust gases heaving the engine  $F = \dot{m} \, V_{\text{exit}} - \dot{m} \, V_{\text{inlet}} = \dot{m} \, (V_{\text{exit}} - V_{\text{inlet}})$ 

The power developed from the thrust of the engine is called the propulsion power,  $\hat{w}_p$  = F Varrantt propulsive efficiency,  $p_p = \frac{Propulsive}{Freezy}$  Input rate =  $\frac{\hat{w}_p}{\hat{z}_m}$

## 1-5 Properties of a System

Any characteristics of a system is called a property

- Pressure, P
- Temperature, T
- Volume, V
- Mass, *m*
- Viscosity, I.
- Thermal Conductivity, k
- Thermal Expansion Coefficient, A
- Specific Volume,  $v = \frac{V}{m} = \frac{1}{Y}$

### Intensive Properties

: Independent of the size of a system as temperature, pressure, and density.

### Extensive Properties

: Dependent on the size such as mass, volume, and total energy.

![](_page_93_Picture_16.jpeg)

Criteria to differentiate intensive and extensive properties

## 1-6 State and Equilibrium

Consider a system which is not undergoing any change. At this point, all properties can be measured or calculated throughout the entire system.

N We can completely describe the state of the system by using the properties

- Thermodynamics deal with equilibrium state and equilibrium implies a state of balance

### Thermal Equilibrium

Mechanical Equilibrium: No change in pressure at any point of the system with time

Phase Equilibrium: The mass of each phase reaches on equilibrium level

Chemical Equilibrium: No change in chemical composition of the system with tim

## 1-7 Processes and Cycles

It should be pointed out that a quasi-equilibrium process is an idealized process and is not a

true representation of an actual process.

Many actual processes closely approximate it and they can be modeled as quasi-equilibrium

with negligible error. & Approximation and Assumption

Process diagram, which is plotted by employing thermodynamic properties as coordinate, are

very useful in visualizing the processes.

P-V Diagram

Process is the change that a system undergoes from one equilibrium state to another equilibrium.

Path is the series of states through which a system passes during a process.

Quasi-equilibrium physically means that the system remains infinitesimally close to an equilibrium state at all time when a process proceeds.

### 2-1 Pure Substance

Pure substance is a substance, which has a fixed chemical composition throughout

- Substances exist in different phases
- Three principal phase > Solid, Liquid, and Gas

# 2-5 The ideal-gas equation of state

- Equation of state is any equation that relates the pressure, temperature, and specific volume of a substance.
- $\blacktriangleright$  The ideal-gas equation of state predicts the  $P-\nu-T$  behavior of a gas quite accurately within some property selected region.

### 1662, Robert Boyle

$$p \sim \frac{1}{V}$$

1802 J. Charles

 $V \sim T$ 

 $P_V = RT$ : Ideal-gas equation of state

where R, P, T, and  $\nu$  are gas constant, absolute pressure, absolute temperature, and specific volume, respectively.

- The gas constant, R, is different for each gas  $\Rightarrow R = \frac{R_u}{M}$
- $R_{\nu}$  is the universal gas constant, M is the molar mass (Molecular Weight)  $Ru = 8.314kJ/kmole \cdot K = 8.314kPa \cdot m^3/kmole \cdot K = 0.08314bar \cdot m^3/kmole \cdot K$
- The Molar mass, M, can simply be defined as the mass of one mole
- Ex1) M = [g/gmole] = [kg/kmole]
- Ex2) when we say the molar mass of nitrogen is 28, it simply means the mass of kmole of nitrogen is 28kg
- Ex3) m = MN [kg] where N is the mole number

## Ideal-gas equation of state

$$Pv = RT$$

$$PV = mRT$$
 where  $v = \frac{V}{m}$ ,  $m = MN$   
 $PV = MNRT = NR_nT$  where  $R_n = MR$ 

$$P\overline{v} = R_{_{\rm H}}T$$
 where  $\overline{v} = \frac{V}{N}$  is the molar specific volume

- The ideal-gas relation given above closely approximately the  $P-\nu-T$  behavior of real gases at low densities
- → Air, Nitrogen, Oxgen, Hydrogen, Helium, Argon, Neon, ...
- Dense gases such as water vapor should not be treated as ideal gases.

![](_page_99_Figure_22.jpeg)

2.6 Compressibility Factor

Chis deviation from ideal-gas behavior at a given temperature and pressure can accurately be accounted for the introduction of a correction factor called the Compressibility Factor, Z, defined as

$$=\frac{Pv}{\rho T}, Z=\frac{v_{cental}}{v}$$

- If Z = 1, it is ideal gas. The farther away Z is from unity, the more the gas deviates from ideal-gas behavior.
- Gases behave differently at a given temperature and pressure, but they behave very much the same at temperature and pressure normalized with respect to their critical temperature and pressure.

$$P_{R}=\frac{P}{P_{CR}}$$
 (Reduced Pressure),  $T_{R}=\frac{T}{T_{CR}}$  (Reduced Temperature)

The principle of corresponding states

. The Z factor for all gases is approximately the same at the same reduced pressure and temperature.

![](_page_100_Figure_9.jpeg)

From the generalized compressibility chart

- 1. At very low pressure ( $P_R \ll 1$ ), the gases behave as an ideal gas, regardless of removemine
- 2. At high temperature ( $T_n > 2$ ), ideal-gas behavior can be assumed with good accuracy regardless of pressure.
- 3. The deviation of a gas from ideal-gas behavior is greatest in the vicinity of critical point.

## 2-7 Other Equation of State

1) Van der Waals Equation of State

- The equation was proposed in 1873
- It has two constants that are determined from the behavior of a substance at the critical

point

$$\left(P + \frac{a}{v^2}\right)(v - b) = RT$$

Two Effects

- Intermolecular attraction forces  $\Rightarrow \frac{a}{v^2}$
- Volume occupied by the molecules themselves \$\infty\$ b

In order to determine  $\,a,\,\,b$  , the inflection point, critical point is used as follows:

$$\left(\frac{\partial P}{\partial v}\right)_{T=T_{\rm CR}} = 0, \; \left(\frac{\partial^2 P}{\partial v^2}\right)_{T=T_{\rm CR}} = 0 \;\; \clubsuit \;\; a = \frac{27R^2T_{\rm CR}^2}{64P_{\rm CR}}, \;\; b = \frac{RT_{\rm CR}}{8P_{\rm CR}}$$

: The Virial equation of state is expressed in a series form as

$$P = \frac{RT}{\nu} + \frac{a(T)}{\nu^2} + \frac{b(T)}{\nu^3} + \frac{c(T)}{\nu^4} + \dots$$

where a(T), b(T), c(T)... are virial coefficients.

- These coefficient can be determined experimentally or theoretically from statistical mechanics.
- The  $P-\nu-T$  behavior of a substance can be represented accurately with the virial equation of state over a wider range by including a sufficient number of terms

# Chapter 3 Energy Transfer by Heat, Work, and Mass

- Energy can be transferred to or form a closed system in two distinct forms: heat and work. For control volume, energy can also be transferred by mass.
- h + ke + pe = u + Pv + ke + pe, represents the energy of a flowing fluid per unit of its mass.
- The conservation of mass principle

### 3-1 Heat Transfer

- Energy can cross the boundary of a closed system in two distinct forms: Heat and Mass.
- 2): Heat is defined as the forms of energy that is transferred between two systems (or a system and its surroundings) by virtue of a temperature difference.
- 3) Adiabatic process is a process during which there is no heat transfer
- 4) The amount of heat transferred during the process between two states is denoted by  $Q_{13}$  or  $\mathcal Q$

→ Heat Transfer Rate, Q [kJ/sec]

$$\Rightarrow Q = \int_{0}^{\infty} Q dt = Q \Delta t$$

## Heat Transfer Mechanism

- Conduction: the transfer of energy from the more energetic properties of a substance to the adjacent less energetic ones as a result of interaction between particles.
- Convection: the transfer of energy between a solid surface and the adjacent fluid that is in motion.
- 3. Radiation: the transfer of energy due to the emission of electromagnetic waves (or

photons).

1) The work is the energy transfer associated with a force acting through a distance

- 3) The work done per unit time is called power
- ₩[kJ/sec]

- The generally accepted formal sign convention for heat and work interactions is as follows: Heat transfer to a system and work done by a system is positive

# Chapter 4 The First Law of Thermodynamics

- The first law of thermodynamics is simply a statement of the conservation of energy principle
- Energy balance for closed system and steady-flow system (Open System)
- Energy balance for unsteady flow processes

# 4-1 The First law of thermodynamics

The Conservation of Energy Principle

Energy can be neither created nor destroyed; it can only change forms

### Energy Balance

The net change (Increase or Decrease) in the total energy of the system during a process is equal to the difference between the total energy entering and the total leaving the system during that processes.

## $E_{tn} - E_{out} = \Delta E_{System}$

→ This is the energy balance

# Energy Change of a System, $\Delta E_{System}$

. The determination of the energy change of a system during a process involves the evaluation of the energy of the system at the beginning and at the end of the process

$$\Delta E_{\text{System}} = E_{\text{final}} - E_{\text{initial}}$$

compressible systems) the change in the total energy of a systems during a process is In the absence of electric, magnetic, and surface tension effects (i.e. for simple the sum of the changes in its internal, kinetic, and potential energies

$$\Delta E_{System} = \Delta U + \Delta KE + \Delta PE \\ = \left( m_{Interel} \mu_{Interel} - m_{interel} \mu_{Interel} \right) + \left( \frac{1}{2} m_{Interel} V_{Interel}^2 - \frac{1}{2} m_{Interel} V_{Interel}^2 \right) + \left( m_{Interel} R_{Interel} Z_{Interel} - m_{Interel} R_{Interel} Z_{Interel} \right)$$

For stationary system (  $\Delta KE = \Delta PE = 0$  )

$$\Delta E_{\text{Surrow}} = \Delta U = \left( m_{\text{final}} u_{\text{final}} - m_{\text{titilal}} u_{\text{initial}} \right)$$

Energy Change of a System,  $\Delta E_{System}$ 

: Heat, Work, Mass

$$E_{in} - E_{out} = (Q_{in} - Q_{out}) + (W_{in} - W_{out}) + (E_{mass,in} - E_{mass,out})$$

Ex) Cooling of Hot Fluid Tank (simple compressible, and stationary system)

# 4-3 Energy balance for steady-flow system

A large number of engineering devices such as turbines, compressor, and nozzles are classified as steady-flow devices.

 Steady-flow process is a process during which a fluid flows through a control volume steadily.

![](_page_108_Picture_6.jpeg)

- During a steady-flow process, no intensive or extensive properties within the control
  volume change with time
- Mass balance for steady-flow systems

$$m = m_{out}$$

if Multi inlet and exits  $\Rightarrow \sum \dot{m}_i = \sum \dot{m}_e$ 

Energy balance for steady-flow systems

$$\dot{E}_{in} - \dot{E}_{out} = \Delta \dot{E}_{system} = 0$$

(Rate of net energy transfer in by heat, work, and mass) = (By heat, work, and mass)

$$\dot{E}_m = \dot{E}_m$$

# 4-4 Some steady-flow engineering devices

# Steam Power Plant (Turbines, Compressors, Heat Exchangers, and Pumps)

Operate nonstop for month. Therefore, these devices can be conveniently analyzed as steady-flow devices.

## 1. Nozzles and Devices

Nozzle is a device that increases the velocity of a fluid at the expanse of pressure. The cross-sectional area of a nozzle decreases in the flow direction for subsonic flows and increases for supersonic flow.

Diffuser is a device that increase the pressure of a fluid by slowing it down.

The Assumption of a Nozzle and a Diffuser

- No heat transfer rate between a nozzle or a diffuser and surrounding ( $\dot{Q}=0$ )
- No work (W = 0)
- Negligible potential energy (  $\Delta PE = 0$  )

Turbine is a device that derives the electric generator

Compressors as well as pumps are devices used to increase the pressure of a fluid.

- Compressor is capable of compressing the gas
- Pump handle liquids instead of gas

Assumption of a compressor and a turbine

- Negligible heat transfer ( $\dot{Q} = 0$ )
- Negligible Potential Energy ( $\Delta PE = 0$ )
- . Negligible Kinetic Energy  $\Delta KE = 0$

. The fuel velocities encountered in most turbines are very high and fluid experiences a significant change in its kinetic energy. However this change is usually very small relative to the change in enthalpy. So it is often disregarded.

## 5. Pipe and Duct Flow

Flow through a pipe or a duct usually satisfies the steady-flow conditions as a steady-flow process.

![](_page_112_Picture_2.jpeg)

Example: Electric Heating of Air in a House

: The electric heating system used in many houses consists of a simple duct with resistance

![](_page_112_Figure_5.jpeg)

Determine the exit temperature of air?

- -A steady-flow process  $\rightharpoonup \Delta m_{CV} = \Delta E_{CV} = 0$
- -Air is an ideal gas
- $-\Delta KE = \Delta PE = 0$

$$\dot{E}_{in} = \dot{E}_{out}$$

$$\dot{W}_{e,m} + \dot{m}h_1 = \dot{Q}_{out} + \dot{m}h_2 + \dot{W}_{e,m} - \dot{Q}_{out} = \dot{m}(h_2 - h_1) = \dot{m}C_p(T_2 - T_1)$$

From ideal gas 
$$\Rightarrow v_1 = \frac{RT_1}{P_1} \Rightarrow \dot{m} = \frac{\dot{V_1}}{v_1} \Rightarrow T_2 = T_1 + \frac{\dot{W}_{e,in} - \dot{Q}_{out}}{\dot{m}C_p} = 21.9^{\circ}C$$

# 4-5 Energy balance for unsteady-flow processes

- During a steady-flow process, no change occur within the control volume
- Many processes of interest, involve changes within the control volume with time. Such processes are called *unsteady-flow*, or *translent-flow* processes.
- Some familiar unsteady-flow processes are the charging of rigid vessels from supply lines.

![](_page_112_Picture_17.jpeg)

- Unlike steady-flow processes, unsteady-flow processes start and end over some finite time period instead of continuing indefinitely.  $\Rightarrow \Delta t$ 

### Mass Balance

 Unlike the case of steady-flow processes, the amount of mass within the control volume does change with time during an unsteady-flow process.

$$m_{in} - m_{out} = \Delta m_{systen}$$

### Energy Balance

- The energy content of a control volume changes with time during an unsteady-flow process.
- The magnitude of change depends on the amount of energy transfer across the system boundaries as heat and work as well as on the amount of energy transported into and

$$E_{ln} - E_{out} = \Delta E_{system}$$

Uniform-flow process: The fluid flow at any inlet or exit is uniform and steady, and thus the fluid properties do not change with time or position over the cross section of an inlet or exit. If they do, they are averaged and treated as constants for the entire process.

Energy Balance for Uniform-Flow Process

$$(Q_m + W_m + \sum m_i \theta_i) - (Q_{out} + W_{out} + \sum m_e \theta_e) = (m_2 e_2 - m_1 e_i)$$

e = u + ke + pe is the energy of the non-flowing fluid within the control volume per unit where  $\theta = h + ke + pe$  is the energy of a flowing fluid at any inlet or exit per unit mass

If the kinetic and potential energy changes associated with the control volume and fluid streams are negligible 🕁

$$(Q_m + W_m + \sum m_i h_i) - (Q_{out} + W_{out} + \sum m_e h_e) = (m_2 u_2 - m_i u_i)$$

# Chapter 5 The Second Law of Thermodynamics

- The second law of thermodynamics, which asserts that process
- es occur in a certain direction and that energy has quality as well as quantity, is introduced

The Carnot cycle is introduced.

refrigerators, and heat pumps are introduced.

The thermal energy reservoirs, reversible, and Irreversible processes, heat engines

# 5-1 Introduction to the second law

- Satisfying the first law alone does not ensure that the process will actually take place.
   Ex) a cup of hot coffee does not get hotter in a cooler room.
- The first law places no restriction on the direction of a process.
- The inadequacy of the first law to identify whether a process can take place is remedied by introducing another general principle, the second law of thermodynamics.
- The second law of thermodynamics is also used in determining the theoretical limits for performance of commonly used engineering system.

# 5-2 Thermal Energy Reservoirs

- Thermal energy reservoirs are hypothetical bodies) with a relative large thermal energy capacity (mass X specific heat). 

  Surroundings: Oceans, lakes, rivers, as well as atmospheric air
- A reservoir that supplies energy in the form of heat is called a source and one that absorbs energy in the form of heat is called a sink.)
- Thermal energy reservoirs are often referred to as heat reservoirs.

### 5-3 Heat Engines

Work can easily be converted to other forms of energy, but converting other forms of energy to work is not easy.

**→** Fig. 3-8

Work can be converted to heat directly and completely, bur converting head to work requires the use of some special devices Heat Engines

# Characteristics of Heat Engines

- 1. They receive heat from a high-temperature source.
- They convert part of this heat to work
- They reject the remaining waste heat to a low-temperature sink
- They operate on a cycle

![](_page_114_Picture_22.jpeg)

$$Q_{ln} - Q_{out} = W_{net,out}$$

Working fluid(科賽 条利) is used to transfer heat from and to heat engines.

'Thermal Efficiency is the fraction of the heat input to net work output and physically means the performance of a heat engine.

Thermal Efficiency = 
$$\frac{\text{Net work output}}{\text{Total heat input}}$$
  
 $\eta_{l:h} = \frac{W_{int,out}}{O} = 1 - \frac{Q_{out}}{O}$

Schematic of a steam power plant

- A heat engine cycle cannot be completed without requiring some heat to a lowtemperature sink. ( Condensation)
- Every heat engine must waste some energy by transferring it to a low-temperature reservoir in order to complete the cycle, even under idealized conditions

![](_page_115_Picture_4.jpeg)

The Second Law of Thermodynamics: Kelvin-Planck Statement.

- It is impossible for any device that operates on a cycle to receive heat from a sin

# reservoir and produce a net amount of work.

This physically means that no heat engine can have a thermal efficiency of 100 %, or as for a power plant to operate, the working fluid must exchange heat with the environment as well as the furnace.

# 5-5 Refrigerators and Heat Pumps

- The transfer of heat from a low-temperature medium to a high-temperature one requires special devices called refrigerators.
- Refrigerators, like heat engines, are cyclic devices.
- The working fluid used in the refrigeration cycle is called a refrigerant.

![](_page_115_Picture_13.jpeg)

## Coefficient of Performance

The efficiency of a refrigerator is expressed in terms of the coefficient of performance (COP), denoted by COPR

$$COP_R = \frac{Desired\ Output}{Required\ Input} = \frac{Q_L}{W_{met,m}}$$

where  $W_{met,ln} = Q_H - Q_L$ 

$$COP_R = \frac{Q_{l_r}}{W_{Wel,lin}} = \frac{1}{Q_H / Q_{l_r} - 1}$$

- Notice that the value of COPR can be greater than unity.

### Heat Pump

Another device that transfers heat from a low-temperature medium to a high-temperature one is the heat pump.

![](_page_116_Picture_1.jpeg)

**Heat Pump** 

Refrigerators and heat pumps operate on the same cycle but differ in their objectives

- The objective of a refrigerator is to maintain the refrigerated space at a low temperature medium
- The objective of a heat pump is to maintain a heated space at a high temperature.

$$COP_{HP} = \frac{Desired\ Output}{Re\ quired\ Input} = \frac{Q_H}{W_{rel,lin}} = \frac{1}{1 - Q_L/Q_H}$$

Therefore,  $\rightharpoonup COP_{HP} = COP_R + 1$ 

The performance of refrigerators and air conditioners in the united states is often\nexpressed of in terms of the Energy Efficiency Rating (EER). 

EER=3.412 COP<sub>R</sub>

The Second Law of Thermodynamics Clasius Statement) which is related to refrigerators or heat pumps.

"It is impossible to construct a device that operates in a cycle and produces no effect other

# than the transfer of heat from a lower-temperature body to a high-temperature body

- Both the Kelvin-Planck and the Clasius statements of the second law are negative statement
- The Kelvin-Planck and the Clasius statement are equivalent in their consequence

# 5-6 Perpetual Motion Machines

- Any device that violates the first or second laws of thermodynamics is called perpetual motion machines.
- A device that violates the first law of thermodynamics (by creating energy) is called a perpetual-motion machine of the first kind (PMM1)
- A device that violates the second law of thermodynamics is called a perpetual-motion machine of the second kind (PMIM2)

# 5-7 Reversible and Irreversible Processes

- A reversible process) is defined as a process that can be reversed without leaving any trace on the surrounding.
- Processes that are not reversible are called irreversible processes.
- Reversible processes actually do not occur in nature. They are merely idealization of actual processes.
- Reversible processes can be viewed as theoretical limits for the corresponding ones.
- The concept of reversible processes leads to the definition of the second law efficiency for actual processes, which is the degree of approximation to the corresponding reversible processes.

### Irreversibilities

- The factors that cause a process to be irreversible are called Irreversibilities.
- Ex) Friction, Unrestrained Expansion, Mixing of two fluids; Heat transfer across a finite temperature difference, Electric resistance, Inelastic deformation of solids, Chemical reactions

# Internally and Externally Reversible Process

- A process is called internally reversible if no Irreversibilities occur within the boundaries of the system during the process.
- → During an internally, reversible process, a system proceeds through a series of equilibrium state.

- A process is called externally reversible if no Irreversibilities occur outside the system boundaries during the process.

### 5-8 Carnot Cycle

- The most efficient cycle are reversible cycles
- Reversible cycles provide upper limit on the performance of real cycle because reversible cycles cannot be achieved in practice.
  - The best known reversible cycle is the Carnot Cycle (1824)

- It is clear from the thermal efficiency values that more of the high-temperature Thermal efficiency values show that energy has quality as well as quantity. thermal energy can be converted to work.

# 5-12 The Carnot Refrigerator and Heat Pump

$$COP_R = \frac{1}{Q_H} \underbrace{Q_L - 1}_{-1} \quad \Rightarrow \quad COP_{HP} = \frac{1}{1 - Q_L} \underbrace{Q_H}_{-1}$$

$$COP_{R} = \frac{1}{Q_{H}} \longrightarrow COP_{HP} = \frac{1}{1 - Q_{L}}$$

## Chapter 6 Entropy

- We introduce the second law of thermodynamics and applied it to cycles and cyclic devices.
- The second law leads to the definition of a new property called entropy.
- We discuss the Clasius inequality, which forms the basis for the definition of
- The entropy changes that take place during processes for pure substances, incompressible substances and ideal gases are discussed.
- A special class of idealized processes, called isentropic processes are examined.

### 6-1 Entropy.

- An irreversible heat engine is less efficient than a reversible one operating between the same two thermal energy reservoirs. 
   Upper limit
- An irreversible refrigerator or a heat pump has a lower COP than a reversible one operating between the same temperature limits \(\rightarrow\) upper limit
- → Important inequality that has major consequence in thermodynamics is the Clasius Incompile.

![](_page_119_Picture_10.jpeg)

- The cyclic integral of 8Q/T is always less than or equal to zero.
- This inequality is valid for all cycles, reversible or irreversible.

### Physical Meanings:

In Figure 6-1

![](_page_119_Picture_15.jpeg)

$$\delta Q_R - \delta W_C = dE_C$$
 (1)

where 
$$\delta W_C = \delta W_{rev} + \delta W_{vys}$$

Considering that the cyclic device is a reversible one  $\Rightarrow \frac{\delta Q_R}{T_R} = \frac{\delta Q}{T} \Rightarrow \text{Eq. (1)}$ 

$$\delta W_C = T_R \frac{\delta Q}{T} - dE_C \implies W_C = T_R \xi \frac{\delta Q}{T}$$

 $\blacktriangleleft$  It appears that the combined system is exchanging heat with a single thermal energy reservoir while involving work  $W_c$ . However, on the basis of the Kelvin-Planck statement of the second law,  $W_c$  cannot be a work output, and thus it cannot be a positive quantity.

$$\oint \frac{\delta Q}{T} \le 0$$

- The equality in the Clasius inequality holds for totally or just internally reversible
  cycles and the inequality for the irreversible ones.
- → Clasius realized in 1865 that he had discovered a new thermodynamic property, and he chose to name this property entropy.

$$dS = \left(\frac{\delta Q}{T}\right)$$

$$\Delta S = S_2 - S_1 = \int_{T} \left( \frac{8Q}{T} \right)$$

→ Recall that isothermal heat transfer process (Carnot Cycles) are internally reversible.

$$\Delta S = \int^{\mathbb{R}} \left( \frac{\delta Q}{T} \right)_{\rm lef \ rev} = \int^{\mathbb{R}} \left( \frac{\delta Q}{T_o} \right)_{\rm lef \ rev} = \frac{Q}{T_o}$$

- We have actually defined the change in entropy instead of entropy itself.
- Absolute values of entropy are determined on the basis of the third law of thermodynamics

# 6-2 The Increase of Entropy Principle

Consider a cycle that is made up of two properties

![](_page_120_Picture_2.jpeg)

$$\oint \frac{\sqrt[3]{2}}{T} \le 0$$

$$\int_{1}^{2} \frac{\partial Q}{T} + \int_{2}^{1} \left( \frac{\partial Q}{T} \right)_{rev} \le 0$$

$$\int \frac{\partial Q}{T} + S_1 - S_2 \le 0$$

$$S_2 - S_1 \ge \int_T \frac{\delta Q}{T}$$

It can be expressed in differential form as

$$\frac{dS}{T} \ge \frac{\partial Q}{\partial r}$$

- The equality holds for an internally reversible process and the inequality for an irreversible process.
- We conclude from these equations that the entropy change of a closed system during an irreversible process is greater than the integral of  $\partial Q/T$  evaluated for that process.
- T in the above equation is the absolute temperature at the boundary where the differential heat  $\delta Q$  is transferred between the system and the surroundings.
- For reversible process,  $\Delta S = S_2 S_1 = \int \frac{\partial Q}{T}$  which represents the entropy transfer

### with heat

The difference between the entropy change of a closed system and the entropy transfer is equal to the entropy generation as

$$S_{gen} = \Delta S_{gg} (= S_2 - S_1) - \int_1^2 \frac{\partial Q}{T}$$

- The entropy generation  $S_{gen}$  is always a positive quantity of zero.
- In absence of any entropy transfer, the entropy change of a system is equal to the entropy generation.

$$\Delta S_{isolation} \leftarrow S_{gen} \geq 0$$

- The entropy of an isolated system during a process always increase or in the limiting case of a reversible process, remains constant.
- → Increase of entropy principle
- Note that in the absence of any heat transfer entropy change is due to irreversibilities only, and their effect is always to increase entropy.
- Entropy is an extensive property

$$S_{gen} = \Delta S_{total} = \Delta S_{sys} + \Delta S_{sur} \geq 0$$

The more irreversible a process, the larger the entropy generated during that process.

Example) Determine which heat transfer process is more irreversible

Since each reservoir undergoes an internally reversible, isothermal process, So we can obtain the entropy change of each reservoir as given by

Case (a)

$$\Delta S_{\text{pource}} = \frac{Q_{\text{source}}}{T_{\text{source}}} = \frac{-2000kJ}{800K} = -2.5kJ/K$$

$$\Delta S_{\text{sin}k} = \frac{Q_{\text{sin}k}}{T_{\text{sin}k}} = \frac{2000kJ}{500K} = 4.0kJ/K$$

$$S_{gen} = \Delta S_{source} + \Delta S_{sink} = 1.5kJ/K$$

Case (b)

$$\Delta S_{\text{source}} = \frac{Q_{\text{source}}}{T_{\text{source}}} = \frac{-2000kJ}{800K} = -2.5kJ/K$$

$$\Delta S_{\rm sink} = \frac{Q_{\rm sink}}{T_{\rm sink}} = \frac{2000kJ}{750K} = 2.7kJ/K$$

$$S_{gen} = \Delta S_{source} + \Delta S_{sink} = 0.2kJ/K$$

 $S_{gem} > 0$ : Irreversible Process

$$S_{gen} = 0$$
: Reversible Process

 $S_{gen} < 0$ : Impossible Process

# Some Remarks about Entropy

- A process must proceed in the direction that complies with the increase of entropy principle.
- Entropy is a nonconserved property
- 3. The performance of the engineering system is degraded by the presence of irreversibilities and estropy generation is a measure of the magnitude of the

irreversibilities present during that process.

## 6-4 Isentropic Process

A process during which the entropy remains constant is called an isentropic process.

$$\Delta S = 0, S_2 = S_1$$

The isentropic process is customarily used in thermodynamic to imply an internally

If the internally reversible isothermal process,  $Q_{\text{int,rev}} = T_o \Delta S$ 

## 6-6 What is Entropy?

What is entropy?

Not being able to describe entropy fully, however, does not take anything away from it

nserumess

- Entropy can be viewed as a measure of molecular disorder, or molecular randomness.
- As a system becomes more disordered, the positions of the molecules become less

predictable and the entropy increase.

→ From a microscopic point of view, the entropy of a system increases whenever the

molecular randomness or uncertainty of a system increases.

$$S = k \ln P$$

where  $k = 1.3806 \times 10^{-23} J/K$  is the Boltzmann constant.

# Chapter 8 Gas Power Cycles

Two important areas of application for thermodynamics are power generation and refrigeration.

Power Cycle: To produce a net power net

Refrigeration Cycle: To produce a refrigeration effect.

Thermodynamics cycles can also be categorized as gas cycles and vapor cycles, depending on the phase of the working fluid.

Gas Cycle: Gaseous phase throughout the entire cycle

Vapor Cycle: The working fluid exists in the vapor phase during one part of cycle and in the liquid phase during another cycle.

In this chapter, various gas power cycles are analyzed under some simplifying assumptions.

# 8-1 Basic Considerations in the Analysis of Power Cycle

- The cycles encountered in actual devices are difficult to analyze because of the presence of complicating effects.
- A simple idealized model enables engineers to study the effects of major parameters.
- The Otto cycle is ideal cycle for spark-ignition engine
- The Diesel cycle is ideal cycle for Compression-ignition engine.
- The ideal cycles are internally reversible
- a. The cycle does not involve any friction
- b. All expansion and compression processes take place in a quasi-equilibrium manner
  - c. The pipes connecting the various components of a system are well insulated.

## 8-2 The Carnot Cycle

The Carnot cycle is composed of four totally reversible processes

- 1) Isothermal Heat Addition
- 2) Isentropic Expansion
- 3) Isothermal Heat Rejection
- 4) Isentropic Compression

$$\eta_{\theta,camot} = \frac{Q_H - Q_L}{Q_H} = \frac{T_H - T_L}{T_H} = 1 - \frac{T_L}{T_H}$$

### Physical Meanings

Thermal efficiency increases with an increase in the average temperature at which is supplied to the system or with a decrease in the average temperature at which here

# 8-3 Air-Standard Assumptions

rejected from the system.

 Spark-ignition engines, diesel engines, and conventional gas turbines are fami examples of devices that operate on gas cycle.

## Air-standard Assumptions

- Working fluid is air, which continuously circulates in a closed loop and alw behaviors as an ideal gas.
- All the processes that make up the cycle are internally reversible.
- 3. The combustion process is replaced by a heat-addition process from an external sour
- 4. The exhaust process is replaced by a heat rejection process that restores the worlfluid to its initial states.

# Chapter 9 Vapor and Combined Power Cycles

We consider vapor cycles in which the working fluid is alternatively vaporized and condensed

## 9-1 The Carnot Vapor Cycle

- We have mentioned repeatedly that the carnot cylce is the most efficient cycle operating between two specified temperature limits
- The Carnot cycle is not a suitable mode for vapor and combined power cycles.
- a. Isothermal heat transfer to or from two-phase system is not difficult to achieve in practice since maintaining a constant pressure in the device will automatically fix the temperature at the saturation value. However, Any attempt to raise the maximum temperature in the cycle will involve heat transfer to the working fluid in a single phase, which is not easy to accomplish isothermally.

![](_page_125_Picture_6.jpeg)

- The isentropic expansion process can be approximated closely by a well-designed turbine. However, the quality of the steam decreases during this process. It is difficult to handle the steam with low quality and the impingement of liquid droplets on the turbine blades causes erosion and is a major source of wear.
- c. The isentropic processes for expansion and compression processes involve two difficulties: One is controlling the quality, the other is not practical to design a compressor that will handle two phases.

# 9-2 Rankine Cycle: The ideal cycle for vapor power cycles

Many of the impracticalities associated with the Carnot cycle can be eliminated by superheated the steam in the boiler condensing it completely in condenser.

![](_page_125_Picture_11.jpeg)

![](_page_125_Picture_12.jpeg)

- 1→2: Isentropic compression in a pump
- 2→3: Constant Pressure heat addition in a boiler
- 3→4: Isentropic expansion in a turbine
- 4→1: Constant pressure heat rejection

Pump: 
$$W_{pump,in} = h_2 - h_1 = v(P_2 - P_1)$$
,  $Tds = dh - vdp$ 

Boiler: 
$$q_m = h_3 - h_2$$

Turbine: 
$$-w_{turb,out} = h_4 - h_3$$
  $\rightarrow$   $w_{turb,out} = h_3 - h_4$ 

Condenser: 
$$-q_{out} = h_1 - h_4 \rightarrow q_{out} = h_4 - h_1$$

Where 
$$w_{net} = w_{nerb,out} - w_{pomp,in} = h_3 + h_1 - h_4 - h_2 = q_{in} - q_{out}$$

### Thermal Efficiency

$$\eta_{lh} = \frac{w_{net}}{q_{ln}} = \frac{w_{neb,out} - w_{pump,ln}}{q_{ln}} = \frac{q_{lh} - q_{out}}{q_{ln}} = 1 - \frac{q_{out}}{q_{ln}}$$

# 9-3 Deviation of actual vapor power cycles from idealized ones

- The actual vapor power cycle differs from the ideal Rankine cycle
- Fluid friction and heat loss to the surroundings are the two common sources of irreversibilities.
- Fluid friction causes pressure drops in the boiler, the condenser and the piping between various components.

\_

The deviation of actual pumps and turbines from the isentropic ones can be accurately
accounted for by utilizing isentropic efficiencies

$$\eta_p = \frac{w_s}{w_a} = \frac{h_{2a} - h_i}{h_{2a} - h_i}, \ \eta_T = \frac{w_a}{w_s} = \frac{h_s - h_{4a}}{h_s - h_{4s}}$$

![](_page_126_Picture_2.jpeg)

# 9-4 How can we increase the efficiency of the Rankine Cycles?

- Every effort is made to improve the efficiency of the cycle on which steam power
  plants operate because even small increases in thermal efficiency can mean large
  savings from the fuel requirements.
- Basic idea
- → "Increase the average temperature at which heat is transferred to the working fluid in the boiler, or decrease the average temperature at which heat is rejected from the working fluid in the condenser."

# Lowering the Condenser Pressure (Lowers $T_{low, nv}$ )

• lowering the operating pressure of the condenser automatically lowers the temperature of the steam.

![](_page_126_Picture_9.jpeg)

- . The area on this diagram represents the increase in the net work output as a result of lowing the condenser pressure from  $\,P_4 < \,P_4\,$
- There is a lower limit on the condenser pressure that can be used
- The presence of larges quantities of moisture decreases the turbine efficiency and erodes the turbine blades.

# Superheating the Steam to High Temperature (Increases Thigh.m.)

![](_page_126_Picture_14.jpeg)

- The average temperature at which heat is added to the steam can be increased without increasing the boiler pressure by superheating the steam to high temperature.
- It decreases the moisture content of the steam at the turbine exit.
- The temperature to which steam can be superheated is limited by metallurgical considerations.

ಉ

# Increasing the Boiler Pressure (Increases, $T_{high,ev}$ )

Another way of increasing the average temperature during the heat addition process is to increase the operating pressure of the boiler, which automatically raise the temperature at which boiling takes place.

![](_page_127_Picture_2.jpeg)

# 9-5 The ideal Reheated Rankine Cycle

- How can we take advantage of the increased efficiencies at higher boiler pressures without facing the problem of excessive moisture at the final stages of the turbine?
- 1. Superheat the steam to vary high temperature before enters the turbine.
- → This is not viable solution
- 2. Expand the steam in the turbine in two stages, and reheat it in between.

![](_page_127_Figure_8.jpeg)

$$q_{1n} = q_{primery} + q_{relocal} = (h_3 - h_2) + (h_3 - h_4)$$

$$W_{tarb,out} = W_{tarb,l} + W_{tarb,ll} = (h_3 - h_4) + (h_5 - h_6)$$

# 9-6 The ideal regenerative Rankine cycle

- We look for ways to raise the temperature of the liquid leaving the pump (called the feedwater).
- $\bullet$  One such possibility is to transfer heat to the feedwater from the expanding steam in the counter flow heat exchanger
- The device where the feedwater is heated by regeneration is called a regenerator or a feedwater heater.

### Closed Book (40min)

- 1. Explain the following cycles: (50points)
  - a. Otto Cycle (10)
  - b. Diesel Cycle (10)
  - c. Brayton Cycle (10)
  - d. Rankine Cycle (10)
  - e. Ideal Vapor-Compression Cycle (10)
- 2. A thermoelectric refrigerator is powered by a battery, and has a COP of 0.2. If the refrigerator cools a canned drink with water from  $T_{initial}$ =20 °C to  $T_{final}$ =4 °C in 10min. In addition, during the process, the canned drink has received the heat from the surrounding by natural convection as follows;

$$\dot{Q}_{convection} = h(T_{\infty} - T_{s})A_{c}$$

 $h = 5W / m^2 K$ : Heat transfer coefficient of natural convection

 $T_{\infty} = 35^{\circ}C$ : Surrounding temperature

$$T_s = \frac{T_{initial} + T_{final}}{2}$$

Determine the average electric power consumed by the thermoelectric refrigerator? (Hints:  $\rho = 1kg/L$ .  $C = 4.18kJ/kg^{\circ}C$ )

![](_page_154_Picture_17.jpeg)

### Open Book (50min)

- 1. Consider an ideal gas-turbine cycle with two stages of compression and two stage of expansion. The pressure ratio across each stage of the compressor and turbine is 3. The air enters each stage of the compressor at 300K and each stage of the turbine at 1200K. Determine the back work ratio,  $r_{bw} = \frac{w_{C,in}}{w_{T,out}}$  and the thermal efficiency of the cycle assuming (a) no regenerator is used and (b) a regenerator with 75% effectiveness is used. Use constant specific heats at room temperature.
- 2. Consider a regenerative cycle using steam as the working fluid. Steam leaves the boiler and enters the turbine at 4Mpa, 400 °C. After expansion to 400kPa, some of the steam is extracted from the turbine for the purpose of heating the feedwater in an open feedwater heater. The pressure in the feedwater heater is 400kPa and the water leaving it is saturated liquid at 400kPa. The steam not extracted expands to 10kPa. Determine the cycle efficiency.

![](_page_155_Figure_3.jpeg)

3. Refrigerant-134a enters the compressor of a refrigerator at 140kPa and -10 °C at a rate of 0.3 m³/min and leaves at 1Mpa. The isentropic efficiency of the compressor is 78 percent. The refrigerant enters the throttling valve at 0.95Mpa and 30 °C and leaves the evaporator as saturated vapor at -18.5 °C. Show the cycle on a T-s diagram with respect to saturation lines and determine (a) the power input to the compressor, (b) the rate of heat removal from the refrigerated space and (c) the pressure drop and rate of heat gain in the line between the evaporator and the compressor.

- 2. A supply of 40kg of shrimp at 8 °C contained in a box is to be frozen to -18 °C in a freezer. Determine the amount of heat that needs to be removed. The latent heat of the shrimp is 277kJ/kg and its specific heat is 3.62 kJ/kg °C above freezing and 1.89 kJ/kg °C below freezing. The container box is 1.2kg and is made up of polyethylene, whose specific heat is 2.3kJ/kg °C. Also, the freezing temperature of shrimp is -2.2 °C. (30)

Shrimp m = 40 kg  $T_1 = 8^{\circ}\text{C}$   $T_2 = -18^{\circ}\text{C}$   $T_{\text{freezing}} = -2.2^{\circ}\text{C}$ 

3. A well-insulated 4m X 4m X 5m room initially at 10 °C is heated by the radiator of a steam heating system. The radiator has a volume of 15L and is filled with superheated vapor at 200kPa and 200 °C. At this moment both the inlet and the exit valves to the radiator are closed. A 120W fan is used to distribute the air in the room. The pressure of the steam is observed to drop to 100kPa after 30min as a result of heat transfer to the room. Assuming constant specific heats for air at room temperature, determine the average temperature of air in 30min. Assume the air pressure in the room remains constant at 100kPa. (40)

![](_page_157_Picture_8.jpeg)

- 4. A Carnot heat engine receives heat from a reservoir at 900 °C at a rate of 800kJ/min and reject the waste heat to the ambient air at 27 °C. The entire work output of the heat engine is used to drive a refrigerator that removes heat from the refrigerated space at -5 °C and transfers it to the same ambient air at 27 °C. Determine (a) the maximum rate of heat removal from the refrigerated space and (b) the total rate of heat rejection to the ambient air. (30)
- 5. Two rigid tanks are connected by a valve. Tank A is insulated and contains 0.2 m³ of steam at 400kPa and 80 percent quality. Tank B is uninsulated and contains 3kg of steam at 200kPa and 250 °C. The valve is now opened, and steam flows form tank A to tank B until the pressure in tank A drops to 300kPa. During this process 600kJ of heat is transferred from tank B to the surroundings at 0 °C. Assuming the steam remaining inside tank A to have undergone a reversible adiabatic process, determine (a) the final temperature in each tank and (b) the entropy generated during this process. (50)
